55 Absentee Ballot Counting Manual July 2026  
 
Appendix B Opening and Closing Procedures 
 Opening Procedures 
Tabulator Seals 
1. The Registrars of Voters place seals on the tabulator after completion of the Logic and 
Accuracy Testing process as well as on the tabulator’s container.   
2. Any seal which is broken or placed in the course of election duties must be recorded in 
the Moderator’s Return. Any missing or tampered with seal must be noted in 
Moderator’s Diary and reported to the Registrars.  
3. If the tabulator is contained in a transfer case, there will be one seal holding the case 
shut. 
4. If the tabulator is contained in the box, each access point of the box should have a seal. 
a. The top seal is on the clamshell which contains the tabulator. 
b. The second is on the auxiliary bin above the main ballot bin. 
c. The third is on the ballot access door. 
d. These three seals will be broken when preparing the tabulator. Place the broken 
seals in a secure container. 
5. There should be one visible seal inside the tabulator. This is located on the inside left of 
the tabulator on the compartment holding the memory stick. This should remain sealed 
through election day. 
6. After preparing the tabulator, seals should be applied to the front access door, rear 
access door, and the ballot box tabulator release lock.  
a. These seals may need to be broken during the day to clear a ballot jam or 
replace the tape. Replace these seals after fixing the issue. Keep a record of 
these seals and store the broken seals. 
 
Setting Up Tabulator for Election 
 
If Tabulator Received in Sealed Transfer Case Start Here:  
1. Remove Tabulator from its bag.  
2. Slide onto ballot box rails, about 2/3 of the way in. Connect Power to Tabulator.  
3. Push plug into rear of tabulator.  
a. Hold power cord with plug flat-side up and connect it into back of tabulator 
(Make sure it clicks in).  
4. Plug power cable into AC power.  
5. Slide tabulator in fully.  
6. Lock in place. (Move on to step 7.) 
 
   
 
56 Absentee Ballot Counting Manual July 2026  
If Tabulator Received Already in Ballot Box Start Here:  
1. Unwind power cable behind ballot box & plug into AC power.  
2. Open the Ballot Box Lid.  
3. When opening: First unlock, then open latches. (Move on to step 7.)  
 
7. Gently Lift Tabulator Screen to Start Up the DS300  
8. Powers up automatically 
a. If the DS300 does not automatically power up, close the touchscreen and 
confirm the DS300 is plugged into a working outlet. Then, reopen the lid.  
b. If the DS300 still does not work, use the plastic covered key to open the Front 
Access Compartment and press the Power button.  
c. Find more information: DS300 Operator’s Guide, p. 37 to 41.  
 
Opening the Polls for Voting  
9. Enter Election Code.  
a. Enter the requested code, using the touchscreen.  
i. The code is case sensitive. 
ii. Specify code type “Election Code” or “Override Code” or “Administrative 
Code.” 
iii. The town is able to bypass this screen. 
b. Then, touch “Accept.”   
 
10. Green Check Mark on the screen confirms the election definition is found. 
11. The unit is connected to the AC. 
a. Confirm the public count is zero. 
b. Confirm the date, time, election, and poll name are correct.  
12. On touch screen tap: “Open Poll.” 
a. Wait for the default election configuration report to print. 
b. The configuration report is kept with the Moderator’s Return. 
13. During the early voting period, three tapes are printed each morning before polls open 
and evening after the end of voting each night. 
a. During early voting, the printed reports are “Daily Ballot Status Accounting.”  
i. This is to prevent candidate and result information from being released 
prior to the close of polls on election day. 
ii. This contains only ballot count information.  
b. During early voting, print “Voting Results” or “Precinct Voting Reports” only 
occurs at the end of the election after the close of polls. 
14. On election day when preparing the tabulator for this single voting day, print zero 
reports tape. This report will allow the moderator to view the candidates and questions 
on the ballot.  
a. Tap “Report Options.” 
b. Verify the format is “Public.” 
c. The report tapes are the “Precinct Voting Reports.” 
d. Tap “Print Report.” 
   
 
57 Absentee Ballot Counting Manual July 2026  
e. Print as many as needed, but no less than three. 
f. The first tape is kept in the Moderator’s Return. 
i. The tape is attached to the “Tabulator Tapes” page. 
g. The second tape is posted for public viewing. 
h. The third tape is stored with that day’s ballots.  
15. Sign all tapes. 
16. Tap “Go to Voting Mode.” 
17. Open Main Ballot Compartment door.  
a. Ensure it is empty  
b. Close and lock compartment door   
18. Open Auxiliary Compartment door. 
a. Ensure it is empty. 
b. Close and lock door with slot open. 
i. When closing the auxiliary compartment door, the slot flap must remain 
open. 
19. Complete opening polls paperwork.  
20. Confirm that touchscreen is ready for the first voter.  
 
 
  
   
 
58 Absentee Ballot Counting Manual July 2026  
  
Early Voting 
1. Early voting 
a. During early voting, the polls are not closed until the close of polls on the 
primary or election day.  
i. Instead, select “tools.” Enter the election code.  
ii. Print three daily ballot status reports each evening of early voting. 
Following the previous step for three tapes.  
iii. Shut down the tabulator for the evening. 
1. Do not press close polls. 
2. Select Admin. Then, press “Shutdown.” 
b. The tabulator is stored in a secure location which is locked and limited in access. 
Follow the guidance of the registrars for storing the tabulator during early voting. 
Auxiliary Compartment, Process Ballots  
 
1. Open auxiliary compartment door. 
2. Remove any ballots. 
3. Feed the ballots into the tabulator. 
a. If ballots are rejected by the tabulator, review ballot for issues. 
4. Overvotes 
a. If rejected as an overvote, determine if the ballot has a true overvote. A true 
overvote has more candidates selected than allowed. 
i. In the case of an overvote, feed into tabulator for overvote 
determination. 
ii. Tap “Cast” 
iii. The tabulator overrides the overvote to count the other contests. 
b. If not a true overvote, the ballot will be hand counted.  
i. The number of candidates selected, not the number of ovals marked is 
counted. 
ii. Voter intent governs. 
5. Blank voted ballots 
a. If there are no markings on the ballot, feed into tabulator. 
b. Tap “Cast” not an overvote. 
c. If there are improper markings, the assistant registrars look for voter intent. In a 
disagreement, the moderator breaks the tie. 
i. Hand count will be required, if intent is determined. 
ii. Any hand counted ballots are placed in a marked envelope labeled 
“auxiliary bin.” The envelope is placed in the ballot transfer case. 
d. Party designation is ignored.  
6. Final results tape 
a. After the final results tape prints, a “Write-In Review Report” will automatically 
generate. 
   
 
59 Absentee Ballot Counting Manual July 2026  
i. This report is provided to the Head Moderator for verification. 
ii. Verification ensures that the write-in vote for a candidate already listed 
on the ballot was not counted by the tabulator. 
iii. This report includes images of the write-in and must be stored securely 
with the ballots. The handwriting could compromise voter privacy. 
 
Closing the Tabulator  
 
1. Open the front access compartment on tabulator. 
2. Press and release close button. 
3. Tap “Close Poll” on the screen to end voting. 
4. You will see “Closing poll. Please wait...” on the screen. 
5. One long tape will print at least three results reports tapes. 
6. Sign each results tape. 
a. Attach the first tape to the “Tabulator Tapes” page in Moderator’s Return. 
b. The second tape is posted for public viewing. 
c. The third tape is stored with the ballots. 
7. The write-in report 
a. The write-in report will generate automatically. 
b. Detach from any other report. 
c. Review the write-ins. 
i. If a write-in has been marked for a candidate on the ballot which allows 
only one vote, count one vote for the candidate. 
ii. If the write-in vote has been marked for a candidate, preprinted on the 
ballot that allows two or more voted, the ballot needs to be reviewed to 
ensure the tabulator has not already counted a vote for the candidate. 
8. Review of write-in votes after closing the polls as follows: 
a. When the DS300 has finished printing reports, and the following screen confirms 
the poll has been closed, touch Write-Ins.  
b. Wait while the DS300 retrieves write-in images from memory.  
c. This may take up to 60 seconds. (If the number of ballot images exceeds the 
documented capacity of the internal CompactFlash card, the DS300 will be 
unable to compile the list of ballot images with write-in votes. Refer to ES&S 
manual 11.6 Write-in Votes Exceed Memory Capacity for more information.)  
d. The write-in review screen appears and displays the top portion of the first ballot 
image that contains a write-in vote.  
e. The screen also displays a header to inform you which ballot image you are 
viewing out of the total number of ballot images with write-in votes.  
f. The header includes the Vote Session Number associated with the displayed 
ballot image.  
g. Use the arrow buttons at the right side of the image to page through an 
individual ballot image.  
h. Touch the arrow to view the next portion of the current ballot image.  
i. Touch the arrow to view the previous portion of the current ballot image.  
   
 
60 Absentee Ballot Counting Manual July 2026  
j. Touch the and arrows to jump to the top or bottom of the current ballot image.  
i. Depending on your ballot design, each image may contain more than one 
write-in vote.  
ii. To ensure you have viewed all possible write-in votes on each image, 
page through the entire image.  
k. Touch “Next” to view next image. 
l. Use the arrows to jump to first or last image. 
m. When you have finished your review of the write-in votes, touch “Exit.” 
n. The write-in report is provided to the Head Moderator for verification. 
o. The report includes images of the write-in votes and must be securely stored for 
voter privacy. 
p. Record write-in candidate qualified votes in the Moderator’s Return on the tally 
sheet. 
9. Results 
a. The moderator should read aloud the results from the tabulator tape to the 
public and election officials. 
10. Closing the tabulator 
a. Tap “Finished-Turn Off” when ready to power down the DS300. 
b. Wait for the power button light to shut off. 
c. Remove the elections memory stick in the front access compartment by breaking 
the seal and opening. 
d. Seal the memory stick in a secure transfer bag/pouch/container.  
e. Follow the registrars’ instructions for handling and delivery of memory stick. 
11. Shut down, close, lock, and seal the tabulator. 
12. Pack and seal tabulator either on ballot box or secure tabulator carrying case. 
a. If ballot box, close, lock, and seal the ballot box cover. 
b. If carrying case, unlock tabulator release lock on the ballot box. 
i. Slide the tabulator part way out. 
ii. Unplug the power cord from rear of tabulator. 
iii. Important: To release the connection the sleeve covering on the cord’s 
end must slide back (against its internal spring)  
c. Detach the power cord. 
d. Pull the tabulator all the way out. 
e. Close and seal the carrying case. 
f. Follow the registrars’ instructions for handling and delivery. 
13. Complete the “Certificate for the Closing of the Polls.”

Note:   
This update to the Early Voting Manual includes changes made by the General Assembly in 
Public Acts 26-1 and 26-24. 
The most notable changes include:  
1. The use of tabulators at the Early Voting Locations; 
2. New early voting procedure and elimination of early voting envelopes; 
3. Restrictions on who may serve as moderator; 
4. New deadlines for 2027 for certiﬁcation of early voting locations; 
5. Changes to the selection of extra early voting locations; 
6. Clariﬁcation on poll workers also working for campaigns.

Introduction 
The purpose of this manual is to provide local election oﬃcials  (hereinafter “LEO”) with detailed 
guidance on conducting early in-person voting (hereinafter “early voting”). 
Connecticut General Statutes (CGS) Sections 9-163aa1 to 9-163cc, inclusive, establish early voting 
for state and municipal regular general elections, special elections, and most primaries, including 
presidential preference primaries. The requirement does not apply to town committee primaries 
or referenda. 
Early voting allows eligible electors to cast a ballot before the day of an election or primary. 
Among other things, it set s various requirements and procedures  for voter eligibility, ballot 
custody, staﬃng, and materials.  This manual incorporates Same-day registration (hereinafter 
“SDR”) instructions throughout since, for regular general elections, towns must oﬀer SDR at each 
early voting location.  For additional information on SDR procedures, please reference the SDR 
Manual. 
Overview of Requirements 
Early voting requirements vary depending on the contest. In some instances, early voting applies 
the same requirements to regular general elections, special elections, and primaries, including 
presidential preference primaries. In other instances, it applies diﬀerent requirements to diﬀerent 
types of contests. This handbook speciﬁes when diﬀerent requirements apply. 
The table below provides an overview of the major requirements under early voting. Early voting 
does not apply to town committee primaries or referenda.

Number of 
Locations 
Every municipality is required to have at least one early voting location. 
The legislative body of municipalities with a population of at least 20,000 
may designate additional location(s).2  
Registrars of voters must designate an additional early voting location on a 
campus of a constituent unit with at least 1,000 students living on campus 
or in housing owned, operated, or aﬃliated with the constituent unit, 
being a state system of public higher education.3  
Deadlines for 
Certifying and 
Finalizing Early 
Voting Locations 
In 2026, location(s) must be certiﬁed to the Secretary of the State (herein 
after “The Secretary”) 60 days before the election or primary. The 
certiﬁcation must include information required for same-day election 
registration location(s)4. The Secretary must approve or disapprove of the 
certiﬁcation 45 days before the election or primary. 
Beginning in 2027, early voting certiﬁcations will be submitted annually 
by February 15. The Secretary must approve or disapprove of the plans 
by March 1. 
Following the annual submission, if any change is made to any part of the 
plan, an updated certiﬁcation must be submitted no later than 7 days after 
the change. The Secretary must approve or disapprove within 7 days of 
the submission.

Early Voting Hours and Period 
Early Voting Period Before a General Election 
The early voting period must begin 15 days before the election and end two days prior. The period 
must be 14 days long, except that it must exclude holidays designated under CGS Sec. 1-4. If the 
early voting period contains a designated holiday, the number of early voting days must decrease 
by the number of holidays falling in the early voting period. 
Early Voting Period Before a Primary (Other than the Presidential Preference Primary) 
The early voting period must begin eight days before the primary and end two days prior. The 
period must be seven days long, except that it must exclude holidays designated under CGS Sec. 
1-4. If the early voting period contains a designated holiday, the number of early voting days must 
decrease by the number of holidays falling in the early voting period. 
Early Voting Period Before the Presidential Preference Primary 
The early voting period must be four days long, beginning ﬁve days before the presidential 
preference primary and ending two days prior. The period must exclude holidays designated 
under CGS Sec. 1-4. If the early voting period contains a designated holiday, the number of early 
voting days must decrease by the number of holidays falling in the early voting period. 
Early Voting Period Before a Special Election 
The early voting period must be four days long, beginning ﬁve days before the special election 
and ending two days prior. The period must exclude holidays designated under CGS Sec. 1 -4. If 
the early voting period contains a designated holiday, the number of early voting days must 
decrease by the number of holidays falling in the early voting period. 
 
10 CGS Secs. 9-225 & 9-226. 
11 CGS Secs. 9-433 & 9-435. 
12 CGS Sec. 9-471. 
13 CGS Secs. 9-225 & 9-226. 
 
8 
 
Early Voting Hours 
For a regular general election or primary, other than a presidential preference primary, the early 
voting location must remain open from 10:00 a.m. to 6:00 p.m., except that the location must 
remain open from 8 :00 a.m. to 8 :00 p.m. on the last T uesday and Thursday of the early voting 
period. CGS Sec. 9-174(c)(1). 
For a special election or the presidential preference primary, the early voting location must 
remain open from 10:00 a.m. to 6:00 p.m. CGS Sec. 9-174(c)(2). 
 Model Plan Part 1: Registrars of voters must always open early voting locations as 
required by law. Registrars of voters should contact facility management before the early 
voting period to ensure that the location(s) are open and ready for voting. 
Early Voting Locations 
The law requires registrars of voters to designate a location in town— with access to the 
centralized voter registration system ( hereinafter “CVRS”)— to conduct both (1) early voting 
and (2) SDR during the early voting period preceding a regular general election. If the registrars 
of voters cannot agree on a location, the legislative body of the municipality must designate 
the early voting location.14 Municipalities with a population of 20,000 or more may designate 
additional locations.  
Registrars of voters must also designate an additional early voting location on a campus of a 
constituent unit of higher education in a municipality if at least 1,000 students live on campus 
or in institution-owned, operated , or affiliated housing. The law currently defines a 
“constituent unit” as (1) UConn and its campuses and (2) the Connecticut State Colleges and 
Universities (which includes the Connecticut State University System, the regional community-
technical colleges, and Charter Oak State College). The early voting location must have access 
to the centralized voter registration system (CVRS) and be certified in writing to the Secretary 
of the State not later than 60 days before the day of an election or primary.   
Separately, the law requires registrars of voters to designate a location for conducting SDR on 
Election Day (see below: “SDR Process for General Elections”). This location may be the same as 
or diﬀerent from the location that is used to conduct SDR during the early voting period. 
Minimum Requirements 
Each early voting location must comply with certain minimum requirements. Speciﬁcally, a 
location must: 
1. be used for the entirety of the early voting period, unless it becomes unusable; 
2. have access to the statewide CVRS; 
3. be accessible to voters with physical disabilities; 
 
14 CGS 9-163aa as amended, Supp. 2026, PA 26-1 Sec. 69, PA 26-42 Sec. 28. 
 
9 
 
4. have space to accommodate curbside voting  with signs indicating the 20’ zone of voter 
privacy surrounding the curbside voting area; and 
5. have at least two parking spaces designated for people with a handicap or aged 65 or 
older who are inﬁrm. 
Written Certification 
Registrars of voters must certify each early voting location in writing to the Secretary of the State 
before each election and primary. Registrars must ﬁnalize the early voting location. 
Contents. The written certiﬁcation that registrars of voters submit to the Secretary of the State 
must include the following: 
1. the early voting location’s name, street address, and contact information; 
2. the number of election or primary oﬃcials appointed as well as their roles;  
3. a description of the early voting location’s design, including both inside and outside the 
location;  
4. a plan to conduct early voting eﬀectively, and; 
5. same-day election registration location information. This includes:
15 
i. the name, street address, and relevant contact information; 
ii. the names and addresses of each election oﬃcial appointed by the registrars to 
serve, and; 
iii. a description of the design of the location and a plan for eﬀective completion and 
processing of such applications. 
Deadlines. The table below shows the deadlines for registrars of voters to submit the certiﬁcation 
and for the Secretary of the State to approve or disapprove it. The deadlines are the same for 
regular general elections, special elections, and primaries, including p residential preference 
primaries. For elections and primaries held in 2026, the deadline is 60 days before the event.  
Beginning in 2027, the deadline for certiﬁcation of early voting locations is February 15.   
Deadlines for Certifying and Finalizing Early Voting Locations

Deadline to

Same Location for Duration of Early Voting Period 
After the registrars of voters ﬁnalize the early voting location, the location may not be changed . 
The location must remain the same for the duration of the early voting period, unless the town 
 
15  CGS 9-163aa as amended, Supp. 2026, PA 26-1 Sec. 69, PA 26-42 Sec. 28. 
 
10 
 
clerk and both registrars unanimously ﬁnd the location unusable  within 31 days before the 
election or primary. If this happens, the town clerk and registrars of voters must designate a new 
location and provide adequate notice. In such a case, we ask that the registrars notify the 
Secretary of the State of the new location as soon as practicable, but no later than 7 days following 
the change.16 
CVRS Access 
Each early voting location must have access to the  statewide CVRS. Access to the CVRS can be 
achieved by placing the state -owned CVRS router onto the municipal -owned network. Any 
connection to the CVRS must be live and cannot be a “static” version of the information contained 
in the CVRS, such as a spreadsheet obtained by downloading data from the CVRS. 
Polling Place Activity Restrictions Apply to Early Voting Locations 
Early Voting prohibits the same activities in or near the early voting location as the law prohibits 
in or near a polling place. The exception to this rule allows anyone performing their oﬃcial duties 
or conducting government business to remain in the vicinity, under certain conditions. 
Speciﬁcally, the person may remain in the area (1) only for as long as necessary to perform their 
duties or conduct business and (2) so long as they are not engaged in any prohibited activity. 
Generally speaking, no one can be within 75 feet of the entrance to the building in which there is 
an early voting location or in any hallway or other approach to the early voting room, to solicit 
support for, or opposition to, a candidate or ballot question; loiter; peddle; or oﬀer advertising 
material or circulars. Additionally, no one may solicit support for, or opposition to, a candidate or 
ballot question; loiter; peddle; or oﬀer advertising material or circulars  within 20 feet of the 
curbside voting area. 
Since the early voting location is deemed a polling place under federal law, it must contain an IVS 
System or a replacement system.  If a municipality is required to provide voting materials in a 
language other than English, those materials must be available as well. Any posters that would 
ordinarily be displayed in a polling place should be placed in the early voting location.  
Optional Additional Locations in Larger Municipalities 
Municipalities with a population of 20,000 or more may designate one or more additional early 
voting locations. While additional locations are optional, municipalities meeting the population 
threshold must make the determination and report that information to the Secretary of the State.  
Additional locations may be designated in one of two ways. First, the legislative body may hold a 
public hearing on designating additional early voting locations. Second, the legislative body may 
determine on its own. If the legislative body creates an additional location, then the registrars of 
voters must begin the work of determining the location and staﬃng needs of the location. 
 
16 CGS Sec. 9-163aa(b)(1)(B) as amended, Supp. 2026, PA 26-1 Sec. 69. 
 
11 
 
Determination After Public Hearing.  If the municipality holds a public hearing, it must provide 
notice of the hearing at least 10 days before holding it. The hearing notice must be placed (1) in 
a newspaper with general circulation in the town and (2) on the municipal website. 
The municipality must hold the public hearing at least 15 days before the deadline for certifying 
early voting locations. This means the hearing must be held at least 75  days before a general 
election, special election, or primary, including a presidential preference primary.  In 2027, the 
hearing must be  noticed by Wednesday, January 21, 2027, and  the meeting must be held by 
Saturday, January 31, 2027. 
Within three days after the public hearing, the legislative body must determine whether to 
designate an additional location and send a detailed explanation of its decision to the Secretary 
of the State. The Secretary must maintain this explanation as a public record.   
The following table provides  an overview of the deadlines associated with holding a public 
hearing.

SOTS Three days after the public hearing 
Determination Without Public Hearing. If a municipality does not hold a public hearing, the 
legislative body must still determine whether to designate an additional location. After making 
this determination, the municipality must send a detailed explanation of the decision to the 
Secretary of the State, and the Secretary must maintain this explanation as a public record. 
 Model Plan Part 2: Registrars of voters must choose and certify the early voting location 
by the speciﬁed deadline and obtain Secretary of the State approval. 
 Model Plan Part 3:  Municipalities with a population of 20,000 or more must determine 
whether to designate an additional location or locations. 
 Model Plan Part 4: Registrars must ensure that the early voting location is connected to 
the CVRS, has ample parking, complies with requirements for voters with disabilities, and 
generally complies with the 75-foot rule. 
Recommendations 
1. The early voting location must maximize privacy for voters to the greatest extent possible. 
2. The early voting location must have all ballots for the entire municipality. 
 
12 
 
3. Even though SDR is available for regular elections, some voters may insist that they are 
already properly registered and decline to go through the registration process. Therefore, 
provisional ballots must be available at the early voting location when candidates for a 
federal oﬃce are on the ballot. 
4. No later than one day before the start of early voting, the registrars of voters should 
contact the facility management staﬀ responsible for any early voting location ; visit the 
location to ensure it can be accessed ; review the status of the location to ensure that all 
materials are in proper working order, and; ensure that the appropriate person is available 
to open the location or the moderator has obtained the key to the location from the 
registrars of voters. 
Oﬃcials and Training 
Early voting requires registrars of voters to appoint a moderator and other election and primary 
oﬃcials as necessary to serve at each early voting location and to delegate to them any of their 
responsibilities. Registrars of voters must train and supervise the oﬃcials they appoint. 
Registrars must make curbside voting available during the early voting period, so staﬃng plans 
should take this into consideration. (Reminder: Under curbside voting, two election oﬃcials of 
opposite political parties must perform the required functions. In the case of a primary, this oﬃce 
encourages registrars to appoint an unaﬃliated or opposite-party elector to assist with curbside 
voting. This is not a legal requirement. 
Early Voting Moderators 
Registrars must appoint moderators at least 20 days before the commencement of early voting 
for an election or primary .
17 Not later than the 14 th day before early voting, the registrars shall 
provide the Secretary with a report of the moderators, consisting of their names, addresses, and 
cell phone numbers, if available.18 Moderators may diﬀer from day to day. For each day of early 
voting, the registrars may split the election oﬃcials into two shifts as they would for a regular 
election day under CGS Sec. 9 -258a, which may include separate moderators for shift one and 
shift two.  
If more than one moderator serves during an early voting day, both moderators should keep a 
shared written record in the moderator’s diary of the hours and time they each served. Both 
moderators should also sign the diary , noting the time of the moderator shift change. The 
moderator shifts should be timed appropriately so that there is no gap between shifts when a 
moderator is not present. The divided shift does not need to remain the same from day to day of 
early voting an d may be adjusted as needed. Moder ator 1 should not come back to the early 
voting location after completing the shift . Moderator 2 should not have worked for a political 
party before beginning their shift.  
 
17 CGS Sec. 9-229, as amended, PA 26-1 Sec. 75, PA 26-42 Sec. 53. 
18 CGS Sec. 9-228a, as amended, PA 26-1 Sec. 72. 
 
13 
 
Election and pri mary oﬃcials are not allowed to perform party services on any day they are 
appointed to serve during the early voting period. Election and primary oﬃcials cannot appear at 
any political party headquarters before the polls close on any day they are appointed to serve as 
an early voting oﬃcial.19 
The registrar cannot appoint as moderator any person who  was convicted, pled guilty or nolo 
contendere to any felony involving: fraud, forgery, larceny, embezzlement , or bribery, or any 
criminal oﬀense under Title 9.20 
Registrars of Voters and Town Clerks Serving as a Moderator  
A registrar of voters may serve as an early voting moderator so long as both registrars agree to 
this appointment.21 To eﬀectuate the appointment of a registrar as an early voting moderator, 
the registrars shall jointly submit to the Secretary of the State the following:  
1. a certiﬁcation that they agree to the appointment, and  
2. a written plan for covering the registrar’s normal duties, to ensure that the appointed 
registrar abstains from any duty that conﬂicts with their role as a moderator while serving 
in the role for each day that the registrar is to serve as a moderator.  
A town clerk may serve as a moderator, so long as the clerk’s statutory duties can be performed 
by an assistant town clerk or another designee.  
Research on Early Voting Use to Inform Staffing 
The Center for Election Innovation and Research (“CEIR”) is a nonpartisan nonproﬁt which, in 
part, conducts research and provides analysis of elections for the Secretary of the State.  CEIR’s 
eﬀorts have revealed signiﬁcant information regarding the participation and administration of 
early in person voting.  Research has found that options to vote early in person have grown in 
popularity nationwide over time.
22 The national trend from 2020 to 2024 saw a decline in voting 
by mail and an increase in early voting. 23 Overall, the method of voting early in person rose in 
popularity among American voters from 3% to 31% from 1996 to 2024.24  
Access to early voting not only increases the opportunity for a voter to participate in elections 
but also enhances election integrity.25 If an issue arises during the course of several days of voting, 
election oﬃcials have a larger window of time to detect and address the problem. Similarly, voters 
confronted with any problem retain the option of voting by another method or at another time. 
 
19 CGS Sec. 9-163aa(j), as amended, Supp. 2026, PA 26-1, Sec. 69. 
20 CGS Sec. 9-229(d), as amended, PA 26-1, Sec. 75. 
21 C.G.S. 9-163aa(b)(5)(A) amended by 2026 Supplement amended by Public Act 26-1 Sec. 69. 
22 Yoder, K., Tan, A., Colen, C., Ong, I., Minkin, A. Martinez-Ruiz, S., & Mann, C., The Expansion of Voting Before 
Election Day, 2000-2026, electioninnovation.org, (June 4, 2026). 
23 Charles Stewart III, How We Voted in 2024: A Topical Look at the Survey of the Performance of American 
Elections, MIT Election Data and Science Lab, page 7, (July 21, 2025). 
24 Charles Stewart III, at page 7.  
25 Yoder, K., et al, (2026). 
 
14 
 
When viewing the larger trends of voters, there was an uptick in voting early during the 2020 
election and a downturn in voting in person on election day in 2020.26 Reﬂecting the ﬂexibility of 
early in person voting, voters can assess the safety and popularity of the polling place with the 
option of returning at another time or by another method. Moving further in time from the COVID 
pandemic, the trend toward casting a ballot in person on election day has rebounded but still falls 
short of pre-2020 usage.
27 Conversely, early voting has increased in popularity surpassing 2020 
levels.      
Previous research and analysis provided by CEIR built the foundation for administering early in 
person voting in Connecticut. 28 Studying the practices and voter turnout of early voting use in 
other states informed the implementation of practices and voter turnout expectations which are 
reﬂected in this handbook.29 In terms of daily use, states reported that the ﬁrst day of early voting 
is highly used. They also reported that after the ﬁrst day or two, the number of early votes cast 
remained largely ﬂat before surging at the end. 
 Model Plan Part 5:  The law requires registrars to appoint one moderator for each early 
voting location. To ensure adequate coverage, registrars should also consider appointing 
two election oﬃcials from diﬀerent parties to staﬀ the location and , as needed, conduct 
curbside voting. 
 Model Plan Part 6: For general elections, when early voting locations must oﬀer SDR, the 
appointed staﬀ should include at least two assistant registrars of voters or special assistant 
registrars from diﬀerent parties who are capable of registering voters. 
 Model Plan Part 7 : Registrars must train all oﬃcials to perform the duties that will be 
required of them and should maintain a list of replacement election oﬃcials to call if 
needed. 
Recommendations 
1. The staﬀ that registrars of voters appoint to serve at the early voting location should be 
veteran election oﬃcials who are also familiar with and competent in using the CVRS. 
2. Additional staﬃng may be needed during c ertain days and times of the week during the 
early voting period, such as weekends or after work hours. Research suggests that the 
busiest days of early voting occur at the beginning and end of the period. 
3. Early voting may increase wait  times for those standing in line, especially for general 
elections when early voting locations must oﬀer SDR. Thus, local oﬃcials should staﬀ early 
voting locations with suﬃcient personnel to minimize wait times  as much as reasonably 
possible. 
 
26 Charles Stewart III, at page 7. 
27 Charles Stewart III, at page 14. 
28 Sullivan, K., Upchurch, K., Yoder, K., Tan, A., Martinez-Ruiz, S., Flemke, K., Proposals for Early In-Person Voting in 
the State of Connecticut: A Report from the Center for Election Innovation & Research, (December 2022).  
29 Sullivan, K., at page 8.  
 
15 
 
4. The registrars of voters should identify a replacement pool of election oﬃcials who can be 
deployed to an early voting location to replace oﬃcials who become unable to work 
during the period or to alleviate oﬃcials at an overburdened location. 
Election Warning 
Under Early Voting, town clerks or assistant town clerks shall give warning of state and municipal, 
regular, and special elections no more than 15 or less than ﬁve days before the early voting period 
starts. By law, town clerks must post the warning in (1) a newspaper of general circulation and (2) 
on the town website.
30  
In terms of a primary, town clerks shall publish notice as soon as reasonable after receiving notice 
that one will occur. By law, town clerks receive notice that a primary will occur from the Secretary 
of the State for state elections and from registrars of voters for municipal elections.31 
Please note that town clerks must still provide the standard notice before primary and election 
day. 
Registration and Enrollment for Early Voting in Primaries 
Existing law allows a person to vote in a primary if they register to vote and enroll  in person by 
noon on the day before the primary. Early Voting similarly sets deadlines by which a person may 
register to vote or enroll in a party and vote during the early voting period before a primary. 
Unaffiliated Voters 
Under the early voting law, unaﬃliated voters who seek to vote during early voting before a 
primary may do so if their enrollment application is ﬁled in person with the registrars of voters by 
noon on the business day immediately before the start of the early voting period.  
For example, if the early voting period starts on a Tuesday, the enrollment application must be 
ﬁled by noon on Monday (or if that day is a state holiday, on Friday). The person may then return 
starting Tuesday to vote early. For the August 11, 2026, primary, unaﬃliated voters who seek to 
vote during early voting  must ﬁle their enrollment application in person with the registrar of 
voters by 12:00 p.m. noon on Friday, July 31, 2026. These electors may vote on any day during 
the early voting period. 
New Registrants 
Individuals who are not registered but ﬁle their voter registration application in person with the 
registrars of voters or municipal clerk by noon on the business day immediately before the day 
they want to vote early may (1) register and enroll in a political party and (2) vote during the early 
 
30 C.G.S. Secs. 9-225 and 9-226. 
31 C.G.S. Secs. 9-433 and 9-435. 
 
 
16 
 
voting period starting the next voting day.  For example, if a person registers to vote by noon on 
Wednesday during an early voting period, they may return starting Thursday and vote early. 
17-year-olds who will be 18 years old by Election Day are eligible to vote during the early voting 
period.32 
Early Voting Process 
To cast an early voting ballot, an elector must do the following: 
1. appear in person at an early voting location within the designated times, 
2. comply with election day identiﬁcation requirements by either (a) showing adequate 
identiﬁcation or (b) signing an aﬃdavit attesting to his or her identity, and 
3. print their name and sign the Early Voting Logbook. 
Elector is Eligible to Vote  
If the registrars of voters determine that the elector is eligible to vote, they must check the CVRS 
to see if the elector has already voted. If not, the elector must be given an early voting ballot and 
sign the early voting Logbook. The registrar of voters must record the issuance. An elector who 
receives an early voting ballot must privately mark it in the presence of the registrars or their 
designees. The elector must place the ballot directly into the tabulator. 
The aﬃrmation that is printed in the early voting logbook must be substantially similar to the 
following and be followed by the elector’s signature: 
AFFIRMATION: I, the undersigned, do hereby state, under penalty of false statement (perjury), 
that: 
1. I am the elector appearing in person to vote early at this election or primary. 
 
2. I am eligible to vote in this election or primary. 
 
3. I have identiﬁed myself to the satisfaction of the registrars of voters. 
 
4. I have not voted in person or by absentee ballot and I will not vote otherwise than by this 
ballot at this election or primary. 
 
5. I have received an early voting ballot for the purpose of voting. 
The elector must print their name and sign their name in the early voting logbook.  
The Office of the Secretary of the State has provided a suggested format for the early voting 
log. LEOs may create their own logbook format so long as all statutory requirements are met. 
 
32 PA 26-42, Sec. 40 
 
17 
 
The elector’s name must be printed neatly to be legible, the affirmation must be present, and 
the elector must sign said affirmation. 
Elector May Have Already Voted 
If the registrars of voters believe that the elector may have already voted in the election, they 
must review the matter. If they cannot resolve it, the elector may request and cast a challenged 
ballot. T he registrars must then report the incident to the State Elections Enforcement 
Commission, which must investigate. Challenged ballots are stored by the town clerk for 180 days 
after the election or primary, and are not counted unless ordered by a court in an election contest. 
CGS Sec. 9-232f. 
Updating the CVRS and Paper Registry List 
While not prescribed by statute, registrars of voters must update voter history  immediately in 
CVRS upon early voting, so that it is clear when an individual has voted early in their town.  This 
procedural change stems from the switch from early voting envelopes to direct insertion of the 
ballot into the voting tabulator. 
Registrars should also use the paper registry list. The paper registry list serves as a shadow system 
and security measure. Registrars should maintain the same list for the duration of the early voting 
period and manually put an “EV” and the date next to an early voter’s name. This is the same list 
that will be used as the oﬃcial registry list on election day. The supplemental list should be printed 
as needed each day of early voting. After the early voting period, registrars should print a 
supplemental list to use with the oﬃcial paper list for the day of the primary or election. 
Model Plan Part 8: To cast a ballot early, a voter must appear in person at an early voting 
location, sign the logbook, and comply with the identiﬁcation requirements. The elector 
must mark their ballot, sign the appropriate aﬃrmation, and place the ballot in the 
tabulator.
 
Recommendations 
1. When a town is holding two primaries at the same time, the early voting ballots may be 
read in two diﬀerent machines, each marked with the party name. Alternatively, a polling 
place holding two primaries  may decide to use one tabulator for both primaries.  The 
tabulators are capable of processing multiple parties. 
SDR Process for General Elections 
Early Voting replaces election day registration or EDR with SDR and makes it available for (1) the 
duration of the early voting period and (2) on Election Day.  SDR is only available at  regular 
general elections. 
Application and Identification Requirements 
SDR applicants must appear in person at the designated location and declare under oath (by 
signing a certiﬁcation on the SDR envelope) that they have not previously voted in the election. 
 
18 
 
Applicants must complete the voter registration application and provide the same information 
required from anyone seeking to become an elector in the state under CGS Sec. 9-20. This means 
they must provide their birth certiﬁcate, driver’s license, or Social Security card.  
If an applicant does not have any of these forms of identiﬁcation, they can prove their identity  
and/or residency by the testimony of another elector, or by the presentation of other satisfactory 
proof to the registrar of voters. College and university students may present a current photo 
identiﬁcation issued by their higher education institution in lieu of a birth certiﬁcate, driver’s 
license, or Social Security card. 
An applicant whose identiﬁcation does not include proof of their residential address must also 
submit another form of identiﬁcation showing their residential address in the municipality. The 
additional identiﬁcation may include a motor vehicle learner’s  permit; a utility bill due no later 
than 30 days after the election; a lease; a library card;  a paycheck; a property tax bill; 
naturalization documents; a recent passport; or, for a college or university student, a current 
college or university registration or fee statement. 
Checking Eligibility 
Registrars of voters must check the CVRS before admitting an applicant as an elector. If the person 
is found in the CVRS as an elector in the municipality, the appropriate ballot and corresponding 
envelope must be provided to the voter. If the address in the CVRS does not match the current 
address of the elector, but the new address is in the same town, the oﬃcial must transfer the 
elector to their new voting district. The oﬃcials must then provide the elector with the ballot for 
their new voting district. 
Applicant Not Listed in CVRS. If the registrars of voters determine that the applicant is not 
listed in CVRS as being registered to vote in another municipality, they must admit the 
applicant through the SDR process. The applicant becomes an elector in the municipality 
immediately. 
Applicant Listed in CVRS. If an applicant is registered in another municipality but wants to 
change their registration location, the registrars of voters  must immediately notify the 
registrars in the municipality where the applicant is currently registered and request that 
they remove the elector’s name from their oﬃcial registry list. The election oﬃcials in that 
municipality must cross through the elector’s name on the list and mark “oﬀ” next to it. 
Best Efforts to Communicate 
The registrars must use their best eﬀorts to contact the previous municipality of registration. This 
includes making at least two telephone inquiries to the previous municipality within a ﬁve- to 10-
minute period. The registrars must keep a log of all SDR ballots issued and track each telephone 
inquiry using the enclosed Ballot Log. If the registrars receive information back from the previous 
municipality indicating that the applicant has not already voted,  the SDR process  will continue 
accordingly as out lined in the “SDR V oting Process” section below . If the registrars receive two 
busy signals or are unable to contact the previous municipality within the two - telephone call 
 
19 
 
(ﬁve- to 10-minute) timeframe, the SDR process will also continue as outlined in the “SDR Voting 
Process” section below.  
Once the registrars have conﬁrmed that the applicant is qualiﬁed to register to vote (by 
conﬁrmation received from another municipality or using best eﬀorts), the applicant will be 
processed in CVRS. The applicant will become an elector in the municipality in which the applicant 
now resides. If the registrar later receives information that the individual has voted in another 
municipality, the reg istrar must immediately provide all information to the State Election 
Enforcement Commission. 
Delay in Communication  
If the registrars can contact the previous municipality and the previous municipality states that 
they will attempt to reach the SDR location staﬀ, but cannot guarantee a timely response, the 
applicant is not required to wait for a response and should be provided with a ballot. The 
registrars must ensure that the ballot is not transmitted to the counting moderator until they 
receive communication from the previous town or complete best -eﬀorts procedures. Before 
delivering the ballot in question for counti ng, the registrar must hold it until they receive 
communication from the previous town or the early voting locations close on that day of early 
voting, whichever is earlier. 
SDR Voting Process 
Registrars of voters must give an SDR ballot and envelope to an applicant whom they admit as an 
elector under SDR and record its issuance. This requirement applies even when the applicant 
comes in during the early voting period. In other words, SDR applicants do not receive early 
voting ballots , and they do not insert their voted- upon ballots into the voting tabulator ; 
Instead, they always receive SDR ballots and envelopes. 
Under SDR, the elector must (1) declare under oath that he or she did not previously vote in the 
election and (2) sign an aﬃrmation that is printed on the back of the SDR security envelope and 
substantially similar to the following: 
AFFIRMATION: I, the undersigned, do hereby state, under penalty of false statement, (perjury) 
that: 
1. I am the person admitted here as an elector in the town indicated. 
 
2. I am eligible to vote in the election indicated for today in the town indicated. 
 
3. The information on my voter registration card is correct and complete. 
 
4. I reside at the address that I have given to the registrars of voters. 
 
5. If previously registered at another location, I have provided such address to the registrars 
of voters and hereby request cancellation of such prior registration. 
 
20 
 
 
6. I have not voted in person or by absentee ballot and I will not vote otherwise than by this 
ballot at this election. 
 
7. I completed an application for a same-day election registration ballot and received a same-
day election registration ballot. 
The newly admitted elector must privately mark the ballot in the presence of the registrars or 
their designees. The elector must place their ballot in the SDR envelope, sign it, and then deposit 
the envelope in a secure SDR ballot depository receptacle. 
CVRS Update 
Before issuing a ballot to a voter seeking to register and vote by SDR during Early Voting, registrars 
of voters must input the information from the voter registration card. The voter history must be 
updated CVRS forthwith, so that it is clear when an individual has voted early through SDR in their 
town. 
 Model Plan Part 9: A person applying to register to vote under SDR must appear in person 
and complete the registration application. To be admitted, they must (1) show proof of 
identity and residence or (2) prove their identity under the testimony of another elector 
or by the presentation of other satisfactory proof to the registrar of voters. 
 Model Plan Part 10: For general elections, when SDR is available, oﬃcials must check each 
applicant in CVRS. Once they determine if and where the applicant is registered, they must 
follow the SDR procedures as indicated. 
Moderators and Other Officials 
Under the early voting law, registrars may delegate to appointed election or primary oﬃcials any 
of the responsibilities assigned to them. Thus, moderators or other election or primary oﬃcials 
may register voters during SDR. 
Recommendations 
1. Each municipality should provide a dedicated telephone (or mobile phone) to each 
moderator at each early voting location to solely communicate information about SDR. 
2. Each municipality should consider providing assistance to applicants who are in line for 
SDR to ensure they are not already registered to vote in the municipality. 
3. Each municipality should consider dividing the SDR process among various staﬀ and the 
location conducting SDR. Processing individuals who have never been registered to vote 
will be quicker because there will be no obligation to contact another municipality during 
the registration or voting process. Processing individuals who may have been registered 
to vote in another municipality in the state will take longer because it requires 
communication with another municipality and will be more time -consuming for the SDR 
oﬃcial. 
 
21 
 
4. Each municipality should keep the SDR Ballot Log (see forms appendix) to ensure that each 
SDR applicant and communication to other municipalities can be accounted for and 
recreated if necessary. 
5. E a c h  l o c a ti o n  c o n d u c ti n g  S D R  s h o u l d  a l s o  h a v e  a  c o p y  o f  t h e  i n a c ti v e  l i s t  f o r  t h e  
municipality. Any person attempting to vote using the SDR process who is on the Inactive 
registry list for the town should be restored and allowed to vote under early voting. 
6. The Secretary of the State will update the “online” voter look -u p  t o o l  o n  t h e  S t a t e  o f  
Connecticut’s website so that municipalities can use this system to pre-check voter status, 
if they so choose, and to act as a substitute for CVRS if the system should fail during SDR. 
7. Registrars should consider having the oﬃcial who distributes the aﬃrmations, preferably 
one with good penmanship, write the elector’s name on the envelope so that there will 
be less concern regarding illegible handwriting. As always, the elector must s ign the 
aﬃrmation themselves. 
8. The registrars shall not appoint as moderator any person who has, in a court of competent 
jurisdiction, been convicted of or pled guilty or nolo contendere  to any felony involving: 
fraud, forgery, larceny, embezzlement, or bribery, or any criminal oﬀense under Title 9.
33 
This oﬃce recommends that registrars create a signed aﬃrmation for moderators to attest 
that they have not been convicted of or pled guilty or nolo contendere to any of the above 
felonies or any criminal oﬀense under Title 9. 
Chain of Custody Procedures 
Upon the close of the polls each day during the early voting period, the moderator shall record 
the number of ballots inserted into the voting tabulator, lock the voting tabulator against voting, 
remove the voted ballots from the machine, place the ballots in secure storage , and store the 
voting tabulator in accordance with the written certification approved, or order for corrective 
action issued, as applicable.  
On each subsequent day of the early voting period, before the opening of the polls, the 
moderator shall unlock the voting tabulator for use and confirm that the counter is set to the 
same number that the moderator had recorded upon the close of the polls the prior day for the 
number of ballots inserted into the voting tabulator.  
Upon the close of the polls on the day of the election, the moderator shall cause the vote totals 
for all candidates and questions to be produced by the early voting tabulators.
 
At the conclusion of each day during the early voting period, registrars of voters must follow the 
proper closing procedure , and transport SDR ballots in the case of a general election, to the 
municipal clerk, who must secure and store them in a manner as similar as possible to the security 
and storage of absentee ballots. The election oﬃcials who are present when the registrars 
transport the ballots to the town clerk must sign the Aﬃdavit of Delivery and Receipt of Same 
Day Registration Ballots: Towns Where Town Clerks Store Ballots. 
 
33 CGS Sec. 9-229(d), as amended, PA 26-1, Sec. 75 
 
22 
 
If this type of storage is not practical, the registrars must prepare an alternate plan and submit it 
to the Secretary of the State for approval. The plan should indicate the basis for the request and 
set out clear chain of custody and storage procedures. At a minimum, the registrars must store 
the ballots under lock and key in a tamper -proof, ﬁre-proof receptacle. The receptacle must be 
locked in a secure room that is not generally accessible.  
The registrars of voters or town clerk, whichever applies, should limit access to the storage 
location and maintain a log of all people who have access to that storage location. For anyone 
who accesses the storage location, the log should show the names, dates, times, and purpose. 
 Model Plan Part 11:  Registrars of voters must provide an appropriate area to vote that 
includes privacy booths. We recommend at least two privacy booths, one of which 
accommodates voters who use the IVS machine. The elector must mark their ballot, sign 
the appropriate aﬃrmation, and place the ballot in the tabulator. 
 Model Plan Part 12:  Registrars of voters must store all ballots received through the 
process of early voting (if the ballot box needed to be emptied) , including SDR ballots in 
the case of a general election, in the same manner as absentee ballots. If registrars ﬁnd 
that storing early voting ballots in this way is not practicable, they must secure all ballots 
in a tamper-proof, ﬁreproof receptacle to be locked in a secure room.
 
Counting Procedures 
Voted Ballots 
It is important to note that, once voted, a voter may not pull their early voting ballot to vote by 
alternative means. The vote is counted because an early voting ballot is considered “voted” when 
it is inserted into the tabulator.  
Ordering and Certifying Ballots 
It is important to be able to separately report and tally the vote totals cast using early voting 
ballots and, for general elections, SDR ballots. Although early voting does not require registrars 
of voters to separately report the results to the Secretary of the State, it does require that the 
head moderator’s return shows the number of early voting ballots and SDR ballots received 
separately. It also requires registrars to seal the copies of the return with the early voting ballots 
and with the SDR ballots for the required retention period. 
Types of Ballots 
To avoid additional costs, we have developed the following suggestions regarding early voting 
ballots and, for general elections, SDR ballots: 
1. A municipality may order a totally unique early voting ballot  that will be separately 
accounted for by the optical scan voting tabulator , either at the central absentee voting 
counting location or at the polls, depending on the municipality. 
 
23 
 
2. A municipality may use regular polling place ballots  that will be counted at a central 
location so the tabulator will separately account for the early voting ballots and, in the 
case of a general election, SDR ballots. 
3. A municipality may use regular absentee ballots and tally them on a separate tabulator. 
4. A municipality may use regular polling place ballots for SDR and hand-count the ballots. 
Certifying the Number of Ballots Ordered or Requesting a Waiver 
Registrars of voters and town clerks must jointly certify the number of ballots ordered for each 
early voting location in a primary or election, along with the number of other ballots ordered. 
The deadline for certifying the number of ballots ordered (both e a r l y  v o ti n g  a n d  e l e c ti o n  d a y  
ballots) is 31 days before the early voting period for an election or a special election, and 21 days 
before the early voting period for a primary, including a presidential preference primary. 
The deadline to request a waiver from this requirement is 45 days before the early voting period 
for an election or a special election, and 30 days before the early voting period for a primary , 
including a presidential preference primary.
34  
During the early voting period, the moderator must keep account, on an hourly basis, of the 
number of ballots used and the number that remain. If more ballots have been used than were 
originally anticipated or a shortage seems likely, the moderator must immediately follow the 
ballot shortage procedure under the Regulations of Connecticut State Agencies, Section 9-174a-
18. It may be helpful to keep each voting district’s ballots separated in packs of 25 to determine 
if more ballots are needed. 
We suggest contacting ROVAC for guidance on questions about the number or types of ballots to 
order. 
 Model Plan Part 14: Registrars must certify the number of ballots ordered and request a 
waiver, if necessary. Registrars should monitor the number of ballots used during early 
voting. If ballots are running low, the ballot shortage procedure must be implemented. 
 Model Plan Part 15: Registrars must be able to separately report the early voting ballots 
and, for general elections, the SDR ballots received. 
Closing Voting at the Early Voting Location  
A voter must be in line by the time prescribed for closing the early voting location to cast an early 
voting ballot. Similarly, an SDR applicant must be in line at the same time to register to vote and 
cast a ballot. Thus, registrars must place an oﬃcial or police oﬃcer behind the last person 
standing in line at the close of early voting. The oﬃcial or oﬃcer must prevent a voter or applicant 
from getting in line. 
 
34 CGS Sec. 9-255a. 
 
24 
 
 Model Plan Part 16: Registrars of voters must place an oﬃcial or police oﬃcer behind the 
last person standing in line at the close of early voting. The oﬃcial or oﬃcer must prevent 
a voter or SDR applicant from getting in line. 
Updated Emergency Contingency Plan 
Statutes require emergency contingency plans for elections and primaries to consider early 
voting-related logistics , including (1) solutions for SDR envelope shortages, (2) strategies to 
address staﬃng shortages for early voting, (3) a ﬁre or alarm within an early voting location, and 
(4) disorder in or around an early voting location. 
 Model Plan Part 17:  Registrars of voters, in consultation with the town clerk, should 
update the emergency contingency plan to consider early voting. 
  
 
25 
 
Appendix: Model Plan 
Model Early In-Person Voting Plan 
Early Voting Period and Hours 
 Model Plan Part 1: Registrars of voters must open early voting locations at all times and 
days as required by law. Registrars should contact facility management before the early 
voting period to ensure that the location, or locations if applicable, are open and ready 
for voting.  All tabulators used for early voting must have been publicly tested prior to the 
opening of the polls. 
Early Voting Locations 
 Model Plan Part 2: Registrars of voters must choose and certify the early voting location 
by the speciﬁed deadline and obtain Secretary of the State approval. 
 Model Plan Part 3:  Municipalities with a population of 20,000 or more must determine 
whether to designate an additional location or locations. Towns with constituent units of 
higher education with 1,000 or more on -campus residents must designate an additional 
location on the campus.  
 Model Plan Part 4: Registrars must ensure that the early voting location is connected to 
the CVRS, has ample parking, complies with requirements for voters with disabilities, and 
generally complies with the 75-foot rule. 
Officials and Training 
 Model Plan Part 5: At a minimum, the law requires registrars to appoint one moderator 
for each early voting location. To ensure adequate coverage, registrars should also 
consider appointing two election oﬃcials from diﬀerent parties to staﬀ the location and 
as needed, conduct curbside voting. 
 Model Plan Part 6: For general elections when early voting locations must oﬀer SDR, the 
appointed staﬀ should include at least two assistant registrars of voters or special assistant 
registrars from diﬀerent parties who are capable of registering voters. 
 Model Plan Part 7:  Registrars must train all oﬃcials to perform the duties that will be 
required of them and should maintain a list of replacement election oﬃcials to call if 
needed. 
Early Voting Process 
 Model Plan Part 8: To cast a ballot early, a voter must appear in person at an early voting 
location, declare under oath that they have not previously voted, and comply with the 
identiﬁcation requirements. The elector must mark their ballot, sign the appropriate 
aﬃrmation, and place the ballot in the tabulator. 
  
 
26 
 
Same-Day Registration (SDR) Process for General Elections 
 Model Plan Part 9: A person applying to register to vote under SDR must appear in person 
and complete the registration application. To be admitted, they must (1) show proof of 
identity and residence or (2) prove their identity and/or residency under the testimony of 
another elector or by the presentation of other satisfactory proof to the registrar of voters. 
 Model Plan Part 10: For general elections when SDR is available, oﬃcials must check each 
applicant in CVRS. Once they determine if and where the applicant is registered, they must 
follow the SDR procedures as indicated. 
Chain of Custody Procedures 
 Model Plan Part 11:  Registrars of voters must provide an appropriate area to vote that 
includes privacy booths. The elector must mark their ballot, sign the appropriate 
aﬃrmation, and place the ballot in the tabulator or, in the case of SDR, in an envelope. 
 Model Plan Part 12: Registrars of voters must store all SDR ballots in the same manner as 
absentee ballots. If registrars ﬁnd that storing early voting ballots in this way is not 
practicable, they must secure all ballots in a tamper -proof, ﬁreproof receptacle to be 
locked in a secure room. 
Counting Procedures 
Ordering and Certifying Ballots 
 Model Plan Part 14: Registrars must certify the number of ballots ordered and request a 
waiver if necessary. Registrars should monitor the number of ballots used during early 
voting. If ballots are running low, the ballot shortage procedure must be implemented. 
Model Plan Part 15: Registrars must be able to separately report the early voting ballots 
and, for general elections, the SDR ballots received. 
 
Closing the Early Voting Location 
 Model Plan Part 16: Registrars of voters must place an oﬃcial or police oﬃcer behind the 
last person standing in line at the close of each day of early voting. The oﬃcial or oﬃcer 
must prevent a voter or applicant from getting in line. 
Updated Emergency Contingency Plan 
 Model Plan Part 17:  Registrars of voters, in consultation with the town clerk, should 
update the emergency contingency plan to consider early voting.
